### 14.1 Decisions to take first {#paper-decisions}

The authors' decisions, not an agent's: an agent prepares the options below in `paper/DECISIONS.md` and stops there. Several are calendar-bound (consent, endorsement, a journal's turnaround), so they start before any writing. **S, calendar-bound**

- **14.1.1 What kind of paper.** Recommended: one full software paper (12 to 20 pages) describing design, verification and performance, posted to arXiv and submitted to one journal; and, separately and later, a short JOSS-style note if the authors want a reviewed software citation. The two are different texts: a short note cannot carry the architecture, and a long paper is not what JOSS reviews.
- **14.1.2 Venue after arXiv.** Candidates and what to check for each†: *Journal of Open Source Software* (a `paper.md` of about 250 to 1,000 words plus the repository itself under review; its scope and substantial-effort criteria; its policy on AI-assisted contributions, which has changed recently: *verify first*, since it may decide eligibility); *SoftwareX* (an original software publication of a few pages on its template, with a code metadata table); *ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software* (a full paper, optionally with a Collected Algorithms submission of the code); *Engineering with Computers*, *Advances in Engineering Software*, *Computer Physics Communications* (its program library); *SIAM Journal on Scientific Computing*'s software section. For each: length limit, template, open-access cost, preprint policy (that an arXiv posting before submission is allowed, and under which licence), review time. Recommended order to try: TOMS or SoftwareX for the full paper, JOSS for the note.
- **14.1.3 arXiv category.** Primary `cs.MS` (Mathematical Software), cross-lists `cs.CE` (Computational Engineering, Finance, and Science) and `cs.CG` (Computational Geometry), possibly `math.NA`†. A first submission to a category may need an endorsement from an established author there†: find out for the submitting account before the text is ready, since it can take days.
- **14.1.4 Authors.** The list and order; written consent of every person named, including those in `CITATION.cff` who would be authors by virtue of upstream meshio's code (its author is listed there) and anyone whose contribution arrived through the `meshlane` fork; whether upstream authors are authors of *this* paper or are cited and acknowledged, which is theirs to say; affiliations, ORCID identifiers, a corresponding author, funding and competing-interest statements. `CITATION.cff` and the paper's author list need not be equal, but the difference must be deliberate.
- **14.1.5 Disclosure of AI assistance.** A large part of this repository was written with coding agents, and the commit history says so. arXiv and each journal have a policy on generative tools (not authors; use to be stated)†: read the current text of each chosen venue's policy, and write the disclosure statement once, in `paper/DECISIONS.md`, to be used verbatim in the manuscript's acknowledgements or methods. This is a precondition of 14.7, and 14.1.2 may depend on it.
- **14.1.6 Licence and place.** The manuscript under CC BY 4.0 (arXiv offers it†, and it is what most open-access journals require), its figures included, which is compatible with the diagrams being generated from MIT code. `paper/` in this repository (recommended: 14.5.3's freshness check then runs in CI beside the code it counts), kept out of the sdist and the wheels (`MANIFEST.in`, and `pyproject.toml`'s `sdist.exclude` if scikit-build-core packs it: *verify first*), out of the docs build and out of the SonarCloud and duplication scans (`sonar-project.properties`, `tools/dup_report.py`).
- **14.1.7 The version described.** A paper describes one release. Choose it (the release current when 14.4 is run), and from then on the text says "version X.Y.Z" where it states a count or a number; later releases do not reopen the manuscript.
- **Done when.** `paper/DECISIONS.md` holds each decision with its date and who took it, and the consents are recorded there by name and date.

### 14.2 Claims and outline {#paper-outline}

A paper is a list of claims with their evidence; write the list before the prose. **M**

- **14.2.1 The claims table.** `paper/CLAIMS.md`: one row per claim the paper will make, with the evidence (a `doc/` page, a test, a CSV), how a reader reproduces it (a command), and its limitation. Candidates, each to be confirmed against the tree at the described version and dropped if it does not hold as worded: (1) one C++20 core and a Python reference twin implement every registered format in both directions, with the exemptions enumerated ([non-goals](#non-goals-and-decisions-taken), `tests/python/conformance_spec.py`); (2) a single format registry serves every language surface, so a format reaches C, Fortran, Julia, R, Octave/MATLAB, WebAssembly, both CLIs and the MCP server without per-binding code; (3) what each format keeps and loses is declared and machine-checked ([conformance](./conformance.md)); (4) operations are deterministic across parallel backends and thread counts, checked by digest ([determinism check](./benchmarks.md#determinism-check)); (5) readers are fuzzed and refuse malformed input by exception ([fuzzing](./fuzzing.md)); (6) a stated ABI policy with layout tests ([ABI](./abi.md)); (7) measured speed against the pure-Python implementation and against other libraries on stated meshes (14.4); (8) the range of surfaces: the browser viewer over WebAssembly, the ParaView and Blender plugins, the agent interface. A claim of the form "the first" or "the only" needs a literature search recorded beside it, or is not made.
- **14.2.2 What is inherited.** A paragraph and a table stating what comes from upstream meshio (the Python `Mesh` model, the readers and writers that began as its code, its test meshes) and from `meshlane`, and what is new here, measured and not estimated: by file, from `git log --follow` and the licence headers, with the method stated. This is the paragraph a reviewer who knows meshio reads first; getting it wrong is the one error that cannot be corrected by a second version.
- **14.2.3 The outline**, with a page budget (for a 16-page target) and the page each section is written from: 1 Introduction and statement of need: who converts meshes and why a library, 1.5 pages. 2 Related work (14.3), 1.5. 3 Design: the data model ([mesh data model](./mesh_data_model.md), [regions](./regions.md), [polyhedra](./polyhedra.md)), the two engines and the fallback contract, the registry, mesh backends ([C++ backends](./cpp_backends.md)), providers and plugins, 3. 4 Formats: families, the conformance declarations, the vendor result files and how they were checked without the vendor tools ([vendor routes](./vendor_routes.md)), 2. 5 Operations: the catalogue by family in one table, two or three shown in depth where there is something to say (CAD surface meshing, [`fit_cad`](./fit_cad.md), the octree mesher), 2. 6 Language surfaces over a flat C ABI: ownership and borrowing rules, the parity guards, 1.5. 7 Verification: engine-against-engine parity, round-trip conformance, fuzzing, sanitizers, determinism digests, the instruction-count gate, 1.5. 8 Performance (14.4), 2. 9 Applications: two short ones with a figure each (a conversion pipeline; a dataset for learning: [ML](./ml.md)), 1. 10 Limitations, taken from the [non-goals](#non-goals-and-decisions-taken) and from this file's open sections, stated plainly, 0.5. 11 Availability: licence, repositories, registries (those of 4.1 that exist at the described version), documentation, the archived DOI, 0.5.
- **14.2.4 Title, abstract, keywords.** Three candidate titles; an abstract within arXiv's limit (1,920 characters†) that states what the software is, what is new, one measured result and where it is; keywords; ACM CCS concepts and MSC classes (65N50 mesh generation and refinement; 65Y15 packaged methods; 68U05 computational geometry†).
- **Done when.** Every section of the outline lists the claims it carries, every claim appears in exactly one section, and no claim lacks an evidence cell.

### 14.3 Related work and bibliography {#paper-related}

**S–M**

- **14.3.1 The comparison.** A table of meshio++ against the tools a reader would otherwise use, on axes that can be checked: formats read and written (counted from each tool's own documentation at a stated version), languages, mesh operations beyond I/O, parallel execution, licence. Rows to consider†: meshio (Schlömer), VTK and PyVista, trimesh, Open3D, MeshLab and PyMeshLab, libigl, Gmsh (as a converter), MOAB, libMesh's and deal.II's I/O layers, the CGNS and MED libraries, SEACAS/Exodus tools, Assimp (graphics formats), pyNastran and the vendor readers for result files. Each cell carries a citation or a URL with the date read; a cell that cannot be sourced says "not stated". Tools under GPL or AGPL are described from their documentation only, the rule of [how this file works](#how-this-file-works).
- **14.3.2 `paper/references.bib`.** BibLaTeX or BibTeX as the template decides. Every entry is fetched by DOI (`doi.org` content negotiation, or the publisher's export), never typed from memory: a `tools/check_bib.py` resolves each DOI and compares title and year, run in the `paper` job. Software is cited as software, with version and DOI where the project gives one (its `CITATION.cff` or Zenodo record). The literature the documentation already cites is the starting list: `grep -rhoE "\([A-Z][a-z]+( et al\.| and [A-Z][a-z]+)? [12][0-9]{3}\)" doc/` collects the author-year mentions (Shewchuk, Si, George, Pav and Walkington and the rest in [mesh generation's further reading](./mesh_cad.md#further-reading)); resolve each to a full reference and, as a side effect, add a `doc/references.md` the pages can link to.
- **14.3.3 Format specifications** are cited as specifications (title, organisation, version, URL, date read), from the [specification index](#specification-index) once its entries have been opened and their † removed.
- **Done when.** `tools/check_bib.py` passes, and the comparison table has no unsourced cell.

### 14.4 Experiments {#paper-experiments}

What is measured for the paper, fixed before it is run. **M–L**

- **State today.** `benchmark/bench.py` (the curated comparison with upstream meshio that `benchmark/01_benchmark.ipynb` plots into `benchmark/plots/`, and a sweep over every format with `--sizes S,M,L --formats … --memory`), `benchmark/inputs.py` (the `example.msh` bracket and a generated tetrahedral cube), `tools/bench_ops.sh` over `src/cpp/benchmark/bench_ops.cpp` (the operation rows by tier and backend, with `--hash` digests), `tools/bench_instructions.py` (instruction counts, `benchmark/instructions_S.csv`), `benchmark/bench_backends.sh`. [Benchmarks](./benchmarks.md) is a log of changes, each measured against the commit before it: it shows that the library got faster, not how it compares with anything else, and its numbers come from different machines and dates.
- **Steps.**
  - **14.4.1 The protocol**, in `paper/experiments/README.md` before any run: the machine (CPU model, cores, memory, storage, operating system, compiler and flags, the wheel or the build used), the versions of every tool compared, warm-up and repetition counts, the statistic reported (median and an interquartile range, the convention `bench.py` already uses for the median), cold or warm file cache (state it; warm is the reproducible one), thread counts, and that every tool is run through its documented public entry point with default options unless the text says otherwise.
  - **14.4.2 Reading and writing against other libraries.** For the formats two tools share: meshio++ (core, and its Python twin with `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_STRICT_CORE` unset, as two rows), upstream meshio, PyVista/VTK, trimesh for the surface formats; three sizes (about 10⁴, 10⁶ and 10⁷ cells: tier names of the harness); time and peak memory (`--memory`). Extend `bench.py` with the other libraries as optional columns in the way legacy meshio is one today, so that a missing library leaves its column empty and never fails the run.
  - **14.4.3 Operations.** A selection of rows from `bench_ops.cpp` that have a counterpart elsewhere (surface extraction, normals, decimation, smoothing, partitioning, a distance query against VTK's or trimesh's), with the same care; where there is no counterpart, strong scaling over threads on the three parallel backends, from `tools/bench_ops.sh`. Every operation has a row or a reason it has none ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#rows-for-every-operation)).
  - **14.4.4 Correctness as an experiment.** Counts, not anecdotes, each produced by a command: formats by conformance class from `tools/gen_conformance_table.py`; fuzzing hours and the regression inputs under `tests/fuzz/regressions/`; the determinism matrix (backends times thread counts with equal digests); the upstream-meshio compatibility run when 3.18 exists. A reader-robustness comparison (the same malformed inputs given to each library, counting crashes, hangs and clean refusals) is the strongest of these and the one most easily unfair: run it only with the inputs published and each tool's maintainers told of what was found before the paper says so.
  - **14.4.5 Reproduction.** `paper/experiments/run_all.sh`: from a tagged release and a lock file of the compared tools' versions to every CSV under `paper/experiments/results/`, with the machine description written beside them; a container recipe (4.1.8's image as the base, once it exists) so a reviewer can run tier S in minutes. The raw results, the inputs or their generators, and the scripts are archived with the tag on Zenodo and cited in the availability section.
- **Fairness, written into the protocol.** Each compared tool at its current release, built or installed as its documentation recommends; options that change what is computed are equalised or the difference stated; a result where meshio++ is slower is reported with the rest. [Benchmarks](./benchmarks.md) already opens with the section that says where the core does not help: the paper keeps it.
- **Done when.** `run_all.sh --tier S` regenerates every tier S number of the manuscript on a clean machine, and each figure and table of section 8 names the CSV it was drawn from.

### 14.5 Figures and generated numbers {#paper-figures}

**M**

- **14.5.1 Diagrams.** The architecture, layering and flow figures come from `doc/diagrams/gen_diagrams.py` (`figures/REGISTRY`), which writes SVG: add a `--pdf` output (through `rsvg-convert -f pdf`, the tool the PNG twins already use, so the text stays vector) into `paper/figures/`, and a paper-specific figure only by adding a function there, so the paper and the documentation never show two versions of one diagram. Fonts embedded; no figure wider than the text block; each legible in greyscale.
- **14.5.2 Plots.** One script per plot under `paper/figures/` reading only the CSVs of 14.4.5 and writing PDF: no number typed into a script. A common style module (the palette of `doc/diagrams/diaglib/palette.py`, colour-blind-safe, direct labels over legends where there is room), log axes where sizes span decades, error bars from the protocol's interquartile range.
- **14.5.3 Generated numbers.** `tools/gen_paper_numbers.py` writes `paper/numbers.tex`, one `\newcommand` per count the text states, each computed: formats read and written from the registry (the table `tools/gen_format_table.py` generates), conformance classes, operations (the operations table of [architecture](./architecture.md#the-operations-layer)), pipeline steps (`tools/pipeline_steps.json`), C API functions (parsed from `bindings/c/include/meshioplusplus/meshioplusplus.h`), MCP tools (`TOOL_REGISTRY`), bindings, tests collected (`pytest --collect-only -q`, `ctest -N`), lines of code by language (a pinned counter, generated files and vendored code excluded and the exclusions listed). `--check` fails when the committed file differs, at the described version only (14.1.7): the script takes `--at <tag>` and the CI job checks out that tag's tree for it, so a later release does not turn the paper's job red. This is the lesson of this file's own line 3, where counts went stale and were removed.
- **14.5.4 A gallery.** One composed figure of what the library handles (a volume mesh cut open, a high-order surface, a CAD model meshed, a result field), rendered off-screen by `screenshot` or the software rasteriser from meshes built by a script, at 300 dpi; or stills from the episodes of 13.5 if they exist by then. Third-party models only under licences that allow redistribution under CC BY.
- **Done when.** `make -C paper figures numbers` regenerates every figure and `numbers.tex` from a clean checkout plus the results directory, and `git status` is clean afterwards.

### 14.6 Build and checks {#paper-build}

**S**

- **Steps.**
  - **14.6.1 The source.** `paper/main.tex` on the plain `article` class (arXiv needs no template; the chosen journal's class is applied on a branch when 14.1.2 is decided), one file per section under `paper/sections/`, `numbers.tex`, `references.bib`, `figures/`. pdfLaTeX-compatible, no shell escape, no packages outside a current TeX Live†, `\pdfoutput=1` in the first lines†, UTF-8.
  - **14.6.2 Building.** `paper/Makefile` (`latexmk -pdf`), and a `paper` job in `.github/workflows/` triggered by changes under `paper/` and on demand, in a TeX Live container, uploading the PDF as an artifact; never part of the release workflows.
  - **14.6.3 The arXiv tarball.** `make -C paper arxiv` writes `arxiv.tar.gz` holding only what arXiv needs: the `.tex` files, the generated `.bbl` (arXiv has not always run BibTeX or Biber: *verify first* what it does now and include the `.bbl` either way†), figures as PDF or PNG, no auxiliary files, no absolute paths, a `00README` only if the build needs directions†; and compiles it in an empty directory to prove it is self-contained. Check the size limits and the accepted TeX Live version† at submission time.
  - **14.6.4 Text checks**, advisory first: `chktex`; a spell check with a project word list; a check that every `\ref` and `\cite` resolves (warnings as errors); every figure and table referenced in the text; no number in the prose that is not a `numbers.tex` macro or a value in a table drawn from a CSV (a grep for digits in the section files, with an allow-list for years and section numbers).
  - **14.6.5 Accessibility and metadata.** PDF title, authors and keywords set through `hyperref`; alt text for figures where the class supports it; links coloured, not boxed.
- **Done when.** The `paper` job builds the PDF and the arXiv tarball from a clean checkout and the tarball compiles alone.

### 14.7 Review and submission {#paper-submission}

**S, calendar-bound**

- **14.7.1 Internal review.** A checklist in `paper/REVIEW.md`, signed off by each author: every claim of 14.2.1 is in the text as worded there and no stronger; every number regenerates (14.5.3, 14.4.5); 14.2.2's attribution has been read by someone who knows upstream meshio; limitations are in the paper and not only here; the disclosure of 14.1.5 is present; each comparison has been run at the compared tool's current release; no fixture or figure is used beyond its licence. Then one outside reader from the field before posting, if the authors can find one.
- **14.7.2 Posting to arXiv.** By an author, from their own account: this is not an agent's step. The metadata prepared in `paper/arxiv_metadata.md` for pasting: title, authors as arXiv formats them, the abstract (plain text, within the limit, no LaTeX beyond what arXiv renders†), comments ("N pages, M figures; software at `<URL>`"), primary and cross-list categories (14.1.3), MSC and ACM classes, the licence (14.1.6), the report number or DOI fields left empty. Ancillary files†: the reproduction scripts' archive, or a pointer to the Zenodo record if size forbids. Read arXiv's own preview PDF before confirming: its TeX run, not the local one, is the published one.
- **14.7.3 After the identifier exists.** In one change: `preferred-citation` (type `article`, the arXiv identifier and its DOI `10.48550/arXiv.<id>`†) in `CITATION.cff`, keeping the software citation as the top-level entry; a *How to cite* section in `README.md` and [the overview](./index.md) with a BibTeX entry for the paper and one for the software; an arXiv badge beside the others in `README.md`; the Zenodo record's related identifiers (`isSupplementTo` the paper†); the paper named in `CHANGELOG.md`; the `Citation` fields of the R and Julia packages (`inst/CITATION` for R: *verify first* that the package has none).
- **14.7.4 The journal.** Submission on the journal's class from the branch of 14.6.1, with a cover letter, suggested reviewers where asked, and the code and data availability statements the journal's policy requires; review responses as commits on that branch with a `paper/RESPONSE.md`; on acceptance, a new arXiv version with the journal reference and DOI filled in, and `preferred-citation` updated to the journal article.
- **14.7.5 The short note**, if 14.1.1 kept it: `paper/joss/paper.md` and `paper.bib` on JOSS's format†, written from the long paper's sections 1, 2 and 11, built by JOSS's own action† in the `paper` job; its pre-submission checklist (an OSI licence, a statement of need, installation and contribution instructions, automated tests, a tagged archived release) is already met by the repository, *verify first* each line against the current checklist.
- **Done when.** The arXiv identifier is in `CITATION.cff` and resolves, and the repository at the tag of 14.1.7 reproduces the tier S numbers of the posted PDF.

## 15. Math on meshes {#math-on-meshes}

*Admission: field calculus that VTK's mesh-aware functions offer and that meshio++'s kernels nearly give: derivatives and the quantities built from them, per-tuple tensor algebra, cell measures and quality metrics, integrals, discrete operators, high-order field evaluation, integral curves and statistics. Appended in November 2026 from a review of Kitware's* Math on Meshes *post and of what it is built on: the mesh-aware functions of VTK's Python `numpy_interface` (`gradient`, `divergence`, `curl`, `laplacian`, `strain`, `vorticity`, `det`, `inverse`, `eigenvalue`, `eigenvector`, `trace`, `area`, `volume`, `aspect`, `aspect_gamma`, `condition`, `diagonal`, `jacobian`, `shear`, `skew`, `min_angle`, `max_angle`, `surface_normal`, `vertex_normal`), the filters behind them (`vtkCellDerivatives`, `vtkMatrixMathFilter`, `vtkGradientFilter`, `vtkCellQuality`, `vtkCellSizeFilter`, `vtkIntegrateAttributes`), and the CellGrid / DG filters of 2025–2026 (`vtkCellGridEvaluator`, `vtkCellGridFindCriticalPoints`, `vtkCellGridIsocontour`, `vtkCellGridChangeBasis`, `vtkDGHGradOperators`, `vtkDGBezierOperators`, `vtkDGHDivOperators`, `vtkDGHCurlOperators`). The post itself was summarised from a search result, not opened: re-read it† before taking up an item. What meshio++ already ships is not queued: `gradient` (gradient, divergence and curl, Green–Gauss or least squares), `hessian`, `tensor_invariants`, `compute_curvature`, `laplacian_eigenmodes`, the heat-method `geodesic_distance`, `compute_quality`, `data_integrate`, `data_calc`, `interpolate`, `compute_normals`. VTK is BSD-3-Clause, so its sources may be read; each item still names the literature to implement from, and its tests compare with VTK's numbers (a `pyvista`-gated test) where both are exact. Every operation lands by [checklist O](#landing-checklists). No priorities: the order is the sequencing's.*

Two facts shape every item. VTK's cell derivatives are exact for linear fields and approximate otherwise (the curl of a gradient comes out small, not zero), and so are meshio++'s: each page states the order of exactness rather than implying more. And VTK computes on its cells' own shape functions, where `gradient` reconstructs per cell from faces or neighbours: where a result is compared, it is compared on a field both reproduce exactly.

### 15.1–15.6 Status

*(15.1 `quad:` / `boundary:quad:` Gauss quadrature in `data_integrate`, 15.2 tetrahedral `mesh_operator`/`smooth_field`, `mio_sparse_operator` C API & flat bindings, and the DEC spike in [spikes](./spikes.md#discrete-exterior-calculus-formerly-roadmap-1523), 15.3 high-order `evaluate_cells`, `change_basis`, `critical_points`, high-order `gradient`, and high-order `isosurface`, 15.4.1–15.4.2 streamlines flat bindings, `Streamlines` pipeline step, and notebook, 15.5 `transfer_operator` and `physicsnemo.transfer_matrix`, and 15.6 `spectral_descriptors` HKS/WKS have landed.)*

- **15.4.3 `render` on the public tracer (optional).** Replace `render`'s arc-length RK4 with the timed tracer at unit speed, which changes its pictures: do it only with the golden images re-recorded and the change stated. **S, optional**

### Considered, not queued

- **What the Verdict pass left** (roadmap 15.1, closed): a score for 1-D cells (the `size` of a line is its length), edge metrics for polyhedron blocks (they get `size` only), and `error_size`'s cell diameter (`error_size.cpp`'s inline all-pairs lambda) extracted into a shared `detail/` helper; the metrics `vtkMeshQuality` has that [mesh quality](./mesh_quality.md) lists as not offered (`jacobian`, `shear`, `skew`, `oddy`, `relative_size_squared`, the wedge's and pyramid's) are not queued without a consumer.
- **Vector-field topology** (`vtkVectorFieldTopology`: critical points of a vector field classified by the Jacobian's eigenvalues, separatrices as streamlines from the saddles): waits on 15.4, and on a user who asks for it; the critical points of 15.3.3 are of scalar fields.
- **H(div) and H(curl) fields** (`vtkDGHDivOperators`, `vtkDGHCurlOperators`, `vtkDeRhamCell`: face and edge degrees of freedom of Raviart–Thomas and Nédélec elements): meshio++ has no place for data on faces and edges yet; revisit after 8.4 and 8.3, with a format that writes such fields in hand.
- **FFT of fields** (`vtkTableFFT`): a field over steps is a table, and numpy's FFT on `sequence_statistics`' input arrays already does it; nothing mesh-aware to add.
- **Particle tracking with physics** (`vtkLagrangianParticleTracker`): drag, collisions and surface interaction are a model, which the solver-coupled [non-goal](#non-goals-and-decisions-taken) leaves to the application.
- **SPH interpolation** (`vtkSPHInterpolator`): `interpolate` and `conservative_interpolate` cover mesh-to-mesh transfer; particle data with kernels belongs to the SPH codes that write it.

---

## Non-goals and decisions taken

Recorded so they are not re-proposed as gaps. A decision is reopened when what it rested on has changed, and then it is narrowed here, with the date and the number of the item that took it up; the November 2026 review read each against what has shipped since. It reopened five (surface booleans through a backend, 9.1; decomposed files read without MPI, 2.3.8; the `zarr` and `pmsh` stores in the core, 4.15; an in-place `transform`, 5.14, measured and closed again; vector kernels behind runtime dispatch, 5.15, measured and closed again) and four formats of *Considered, not queued* (2.4.5 to 2.4.8), and left the rest as they stand: the reasons recorded for the native constrained tetrahedralisation, NURBS as an entity kind in `Mesh`, Kokkos device execution, link-time optimisation by default (and, measured later, a profile-guided build), solver-coupled learning, contact mechanics, the copyleft codes and the CAD modeller have not moved.

- **Every registered format reads and writes, in both engines** — decided in October 2026, when the parity tier closed; it reversed the earlier non-goals that kept result files and the export-only formats read-only. A format reads and writes in the C++ core and in its Python twin, so it reaches every surface (C, Fortran, Julia, R, WASM, both CLIs and the MCP server's format capabilities); a core-only path is unreadable from the surfaces that have no fallback, a Python-only one has no twin to check it against. `read(write(m))` equals `m` within the limits the format itself imposes (single precision, a cell type it has no slot for, a concept it cannot express): a loss that is a fact of the format is stated on its page and in the [conformance](./conformance.md) notes, and one that is only an unwritten feature is a gap that reopens a *Reader and writer parity* section here. A new format or direction lands as `AGENTS.md` says (native writer registered in `registry.cpp`, parameters through `WriteOptions`, the Python twin behind `core_declined`, the arrays a reader returns written back under the same names, a `SPEC` declaration and a *Writing* section). The exemptions are recorded in `tests/python/conformance_spec.py` (`READ_ONLY`, `ENGINE_EXEMPT`, `PARTIAL_ENGINE_EXEMPT`) and checked by `test_conformance.py`:
  - **Pure 2-D drawings.** `svg` and `tikz` draw a mesh rather than store it: their readers parse only the structure meshio++'s own writers emit, return the geometry as planar (`z = 0`) and warn once per read that depth, and whatever else the drawing could not hold, is lost; anything else in the file is a `ReadError` naming the construct, never a guess.
  - **`ansys_rst_cyclic`**, which is not a file but the full rotor a cyclic `.rst` expands to; its write side is `ansys_rst`'s cyclic option. **`step` and `iges`** outside a build with OpenCASCADE: reading a CAD model means a CAD kernel, which the `meshioplusplus-occt` add-on wheel (since v18.0.0) and the release binaries carry and a source build finds when it can ([skin to CAD](./cad_fit.md), [STEP](./formats/step.md#reading)); on WASM the lean build is the exempt one, and the [full build](./wasm.md#lean-and-full-builds) carries the kernel.
  - **One engine only, by decision**: GiD (gidpost and its Gauss points are implemented once, in the core), the OpenFOAM case writer and its zone, field and multi-region reading, CGNS's face-based polyhedra (the writer's winding repair is a discrete branch two implementations could resolve differently), the CAD readers, and the `step` writer's merged and fitted faces (`faces="planar"`/`"fitted"`, with `segment`'s primitive method that feeds them: loop walking, vertex snapping and the primitive growth are discrete branches too, so the twin writes `faces="facet"` byte for byte and nothing else); the Python entry points raise naming the missing core rather than guess.
- **What the parity tier left as facts of the format**, each stated on the format's page and in the conformance notes, none an unwritten feature:
  - **`nastran_op2`** writes the MSC dialect only (32-bit little-endian words, SORT1 tables). 64-bit words, big-endian files, NX/Simcenter's interlaced table names and double-precision GRIDs are read and detected, not written: a post-processor reads the MSC dialect with no flags, and whether NX accepts it is a vendor check ([2.2.14](#awaiting-a-licensed-run)). The random PSD, CRM and ATO tables are written as SORT1 magnitudes, as the reader reads them (MSC writes them SORT2, and pyNastran reads only the RMS and NO ones in SORT1); no SORT2 pivot is written. Elements on scalar points (`CELAS3`/`CELAS4`, `CDAMP3`/`CDAMP4`, `CMASS3`/`CMASS4`) and `CBUSH1D` and `CBEND` have no cell type — `Mesh` has no scalar-point entity to hang them on — and are skipped with a warning.
  - **`nastran_h5`** writes a table only where an MSC sample fixes its layout: `CBUSH`, `CGAP`, `CMASS` and `CONM1` elements, and `MAT5`, `MAT8`, `MAT9`, `PBARL`, `PBEAML` and `PCOMPG` values, have no table in the six MSC Nastran 2020 files at hand, so the elements are left out and the values go on the plain cards, each with one warning; the OP2 file holds them.
  - **`lsdyna_d3plot`** leaves the rigid roads' full rigid-body velocities (`NDIM = 9`) out of the state, and writes an airbag variable under a title-case name, since the names of a real file are in no manual available.
  - **`radioss_anim`** writes the nodal normals as zeros: `anim_to_vtk` ignores them and their 16-bit encoding is undocumented.
  - **`abaqus_fil`** writes 8-byte words only, the reader's width; a single-precision build's 4-byte layout has no description or sample.
  - **`ansys_rst`** has no complex (harmonic) results, read or written (8.1 gives them a form once a sample exists): no public sample or open reader covers them, and DPF alone reads them. The nodal coordinate systems are written as `ansys:node_angles` and agree with pymapdl-reader only for single-axis angles, since it applies the three turns in the opposite order.
  - **`xplt`** writes a per-surface-node variable as `FMT_NODE` in surface order, where the reader averages the values a node on two surfaces has into one point value, which both surfaces then write.
  - **`marc` and `marc_t19`**: edge and face sets stay `(cell, number)` field data and side regions are dropped, since mapping them to facets needs Volume A's numbering ([2.2.11](#awaiting-a-licensed-run)); the Herrmann, bubble and generalized-plane-strain types come back as the default type of their shape, and a deck holds no data arrays.
  - **`szplt`** does not write polygon and polyhedral zones: TecIO 2018.3, the newest source available, refuses them in an SZL file, so the reader refuses them by name too and the face-based zones stay with the `.plt` and ASCII readers. Partitioned output is for tetrahedron and brick zones of a static file only (TecIO's own limit), and a series is written whole.
  - **`vtx`**: DOLFINx and ParaView read one cell type per file, so a file of several cell types or parts is meshio++'s own extension of the layout.
- **Generators the primitives leave out** — recorded when roadmap §3.1 closed (October 2026), with the [parametric primitives](./primitives.md#not-generated): generators driven by a user callback (no callback crosses the flat ABI; numpy users build `Mesh(points, cells)` directly, and [`isosurface`](./isosurface.md) on a `grid` covers implicit shapes); scanned or modelled assets (the bunny, the teapot, the bracket fixture — data rather than parameters, each under its own licence); and CSG combinations, under the volume-meshing non-goal below (surface booleans were reopened as a spike and kept out on its measurements, [findings](./spikes.md#surface-booleans-formerly-roadmap-7-8)) — two-body interface and contact fixtures are primitives placed with `transform` and joined with `merge`.
- **MPI in the library** — none planned ([C++ API](./cpp_api.md)); `partition`'s ghost layers produce the halo an MPI assembly in the owning application needs. ParMmg, MMG's MPI layer, is excluded with it. Narrowed in November 2026: reading the files a parallel run left, one per rank, into one mesh needs no MPI, as the OpenFOAM and `pvtu` readers show, and is queued for the formats that lack it as 2.3.8. Added November 2026: this also excludes MPI-parallel writers (parallel HDF5, collective `cgp_*`) from [§10](#in-situ-interchange), whose adapters take the data a rank holds and never talk to a communicator; reopen only with an application that needs a shared file and cannot gather.
- **Solver-coupled physics-ML** — assembled solver residuals, adjoints and Sobolev training, co-simulation, active-learning *labeling*, adaptive remeshing driven by a surrogate, and MPI model-part gathering. Every one needs a live solver (an assembly routine, its tangent, its communicator) and meshio++ has no notion of a discrete system; they belong in the application that owns the solver, the division [Symbolic and physics](physicsnemo/symbolic_and_physics.md) describes. Narrowed in November 2026: exporting the mesh's own geometric operators (cotangent Laplacian, mass, per-cell gradient) as sparse arrays, and the screened-Poisson field smoothing two operations already solve, assemble no physics and landed as `mesh_operator` and `smooth_field` (15.2); the solve of a user's system stays the application's.
- **The Python-only layers stay Python** — `pmsh`, `zarr`, `cae` and `usd`, and the physics-ML surface (grids, point budgets, proximity graphs, datasets, training) are export targets and tooling for a training pipeline, registered in Python rather than the shared C++ registry. A core kernel they call (the neighbour search) does not change that; `tessellate` moved to the core ([tessellation](./tessellation.md)) because the native CLI and the flat bindings needed it, and its `Tessellation` helpers stay Python. Narrowed in November 2026 for the two stores a dataset is written to: reading `zarr` and `pmsh` in the core, so the browser, the flat bindings and the native CLI open what Python wrote, is queued as 4.15 behind a design pass; `usd`, `cae` and the physics-ML surface stay as decided.
- **Polyscope in the release CLI executables** — excluded deliberately, so `view`/`screenshot` there report the build flag rather than opening a window ([viewer](./viewer.md)): the executable stays free of OpenGL, GLFW and X11. Narrowed in November 2026, once v18.0.0's plugins made it possible: a Polyscope plugin loaded beside the executable is queued as 4.8.2.
- **General volume meshing algorithms as native operations** — constrained tetrahedralization of a domain, hex-dominant meshing and general hex meshing from a CAD-like boundary, boolean/CSG, boundary-layer inflation and quadrangulation are each a library in their own right. (Narrowed in November 2026: geodesic distance, once listed here, is not one, since the heat method needs only the P1 operators and the sparse solver the core has, and shipped as [`geodesic_distance`](./geodesic.md); surface booleans were measured by a spike in November 2026 (exact in topology, but rounding its output to doubles left most curved results self-intersecting and coplanar contact unhandled, so no native operation is queued: [findings](./spikes.md#surface-booleans-formerly-roadmap-7-8), though the backend route this non-goal names was not measured for them and is, since the same month, 9.1); and v18.0.0's provider registry is the optional-backend route below, which external meshers and field-aligned quads now take as installed programs: [`tetrahedralize`](./tetrahedralize.md#external-meshers-tetgen-gmsh-and-ftetwild)'s TetGen, Gmsh and fTetWild and [`remesh_quads`](./remesh_quads.md)'s QuadriFlow and Instant Meshes.) The answer is an optional backend (the KaHIP pattern), not a native implementation: volume domain meshing reaches meshio++ through [`tetrahedralize`](./tetrahedralize.md) (v17.23.0: natively by its octree backend, the skin fitted by isosurface stuffing, and exactly by Netgen, the skin kept) and adaptation through MMG ([MMG backend](./mmg.md)); boundary-layer inflation reopened through a design pass for layers grown from a surface ([`boundary_layer`](./boundary_layer.md), v17.23.0), the volume behind them filled by `tetrahedralize`; and quadrangulation reopened for CAD surfaces only (recombination of `mesh_cad_surface`'s own triangles by a matching, v17.23.0), field-aligned remeshing through installed programs ([`remesh_quads`](./remesh_quads.md)). A native constrained tetrahedralisation stays out: `orient3d` and `insphere` exist and the Delaunay kernel is short (Bowyer 1981, Watson 1981), but boundary recovery with Steiner points is where TetGen and the exact constructions spend their complexity (George, Hecht and Saltel 1991; Si and Gärtner 2005; Shewchuk 2008; Si 2015; Diazzi et al. 2023). Reopen it when a design pass shows a native recovery on the core's predicates within a stated size, measured against `tetrahedralize`'s probes and its Thingi10K sample. Two exceptions are in scope. Surface triangulation of a CAD model is native since v17.13.0 ([`mesh_cad_surface`](./mesh_cad.md)): the robust predicates it needs are Shewchuk's, public domain and a few hundred lines, and a planar constrained Delaunay triangulation per face is small enough to own; this reverses the earlier decision that dropped the queued Delaunay item, for surfaces and the plane only. Lattice generation is the other: [`voxelize`](./voxelize.md), [`octree`](./octree.md), [`remesh_volume`](./remesh_volume.md)'s isosurface stuffing and the octree volume mesher [`octree_mesh`](./octree_mesh.md) (stuffed tetrahedra and the all-hexahedral octree dual, uniform or graded, [plan](./octree_mesh_3d_plan.md)) are index arithmetic over a signed distance on a lattice, followed by projection and untangling, with no predicate to make robust.
- **A declared tolerance deciding degeneracy in `mesh_cad_surface`** — `model_tolerances` widens where uses start, how far corners and uses may lie apart and which trims pair, but whether an edge is degenerate (a pole) stays on the global tolerance: an edge shorter than a generous declared tolerance is real geometry, and collapsing it would change the model rather than accept its gaps ([item 21](./mesh_cad.md#how-it-works)).
- **What mesh generation leaves, by decision** — recorded when roadmap §6 closed (October 2026); [mesh generation](./mesh_cad.md) and its [measured-and-parked notes](./mesh_cad.md#measured-and-parked) hold the numbers:
- **`decimate_volume`'s star stays unbounded** — decided when roadmap 5.1.4 closed (November 2026), on the measurements in [benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#cell-volumes-without-rebuilding-the-rings-and-what-bounds-decimate_volumes-star) and stated on [decimate_volume](./decimate_volume.md#cost-on-large-meshes). The greedy order collapses onto one survivor whose star grows, so the time grows faster than the cell count (38 s at 16 cells a side, 24,576 tetrahedra). Bounding it changes which collapses happen: a valence cap of 48 is 25 times faster (0.10 s at 14 cells a side) but leaves degenerate tetrahedra (aspect ratio 7.4e5) and inverted ones at 12 a side, a cap of 72 inverts one and reaches 1.5e9, and a star-size penalty gives better quality (least dihedral 0.33° against 0.06°) but takes 3.5 times as long (9.2 s). No rule was found that is better than the one shipped, so it is unchanged and no option is added (a cap would be a layout change to `DecimateVolumeOptions`, the C, Fortran, Julia, R, Octave and WASM bindings, both CLIs, the pipeline step and the MCP tool). The row runs at tier S only. Reopen when a user meets the slow case, or a rule bounds the star without the cap's slivers.
- **Containers in the CAD mesher** — profiled when roadmap 5.19 closed (November 2026), at tier S: `std::_Rb_tree` takes 2.5% of `mesh_cad_surface_quads`, 0.2% of `mesh_cad_surface_untangle` and 0.5% of `fit_cad_network`, the `std::function` calls of `cdt2d` 0.8%, 1.9% and 1.7%, so replacing its maps with sorted vectors or hash tables, or its callbacks with template parameters, cannot move a row by more than a few percent; the rows spend their time in B-spline evaluation (`iga_eval_point`, 12% of `quads`), matching (`max_weight_matching`, 10%) and the allocator behind vectors ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#profiling-the-cad-meshers-containers)). Reopen on a row where a map shows above 5%.
- **Flat containers in place of the standard ones** — decided when roadmap 5.22 closed (November 2026), on the [containers study](./benchmarks.md#containers-a-study) and the [reader rows with sparse ids](./benchmarks.md#reader-rows-with-sparse-shuffled-ids); the core keeps `std::unordered_map`, `std::map` and `std::set` where it uses them, and no container header is vendored.
  - **The readers' id maps** (the largest family, 74 declarations) are not migrated: a hash table takes 0.9% to 2.4% of the Gmsh, Abaqus and Nastran reader rows, and ids `7 i + 3` shuffled read as fast as ids 1..n (within 3%, instructions and wall time), so a container two to three times faster on the microbenchmark recovers at most 1% to 2%.
  - **The CAD mesher's maps and callbacks** are not migrated, by the item above.
  - **Edge-pair and point keys** were not tried on rows. The microbenchmark favours a flat table (1.7 times on edge counts, 2 times on the weld's key), but the weld already has its own flat table, the edge counts the operations make go through `EdgeUseTable`, and no profiled row put either map above a few percent.
  - **Ordered tables:** a sorted vector is 12 times faster than `std::map` on the microbenchmark, and the sites a row showed to matter are already flat (`EdgeUseTable`, the CSR staging of `clean` and `merge`, `split_interface`'s facet table); the others are outside any profiled row's hot path.
  - **`std::vector<bool>`** is half as fast as a byte vector to set and test. Whether any of the core's 62 is written from inside a `parallel_for`, where two elements of one word race, was not checked; do that check if one is found to be (it would open *Correctness debts*).
  - **Reopen** on a row where a standard container shows above 5% of the profile. Not measured: Clang, MSVC and WebAssembly (the benchmark builds with the others), and `ankerl::unordered_dense`.
  - **No small-angle bound stated on a curved face.** Corner lopping's bound (Pav and Walkington 2005) is the plane's; on a curved face `lop_corners` reports the angles it reaches and termination stays `max_points` and the insertion-radius declines. Stating one would need the paper's argument redone in the surface's metric.
  - **A pinched boundary stays declined** by the flattening of 6.4.4: splitting the pinch into one vertex per wedge makes the coarse mesh manifold, but the face's loops pair the pinch's edges otherwise than the wedges do, so each loop would have to be re-stitched through both copies.
  - **Field-aligned quads stay with backends.** Recombination of the mesher's own triangles leaves its worst quads at poles (0.12 on the sphere at `h = 0.1`); field-aligned remeshing is Instant Meshes (BSD-3), QuadriFlow (MIT) or QuadWild (GPL, excluded), each a library of its own. Since v18.0.0 that pattern is the provider registry, and [`remesh_quads`](./remesh_quads.md) runs an installed QuadriFlow or Instant Meshes as a separate process (0.25 to 0.58 on the same sphere, by seed); a linked add-on wheel extracting their solvers stays unqueued until a user needs them in the wheels or the browser. The core's cross field and quad layout (`detail/cross_field`, `detail/quad_layout`) stay `fit_cad`'s.
  - **Curved boundary layers: linearize first.** `boundary_layer` takes linear triangles and quads; a `triangle6` or `quad9` surface is linearized first. Growing curved layers needs a Bernstein bound on a prism's and a hexahedron's Jacobian (the tensor bound `detail/curved_quad` has for quads, raised a dimension) to keep "no cell is written folded" true, and its mid-edge nodes' directions; no model has asked.
  - **Mesh-generation corpora with non-commercial terms stay out.** The Fusion 360 Gallery (Willis et al. 2021) permits use for non-commercial research only and asks its users to indemnify Autodesk, which an MIT project's published measurements cannot accept.
  - **No numpy twin** of `mesh_cad_surface`, `triangulate_polygon_2d` or `tetrahedralize`: a second implementation of the predicates and the triangulation could not stay byte-identical, the precedent of [`remesh_volume`](./remesh_volume.md).
  - **Healing the model.** Sewing gaps, removing sliver faces or rebuilding pcurves changes the CAD model, which is the CAD kernel's work; `mesh_cad_surface` meshes around defects without writing them back. Narrowed in November 2026: the CAD kernel is reachable through the provider registry, so healing at read time, before the B-rep reaches the mesher, is the STEP and IGES readers' `heal` option ([STEP reading](./formats/step.md#reading)); `mesh_cad_surface` itself still never heals.
  - **Code from Triangle, TetGen, Gmsh, CGAL's GPL packages or Blossom V**, under any route other than a separate process: their licences ([THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES](https://github.com/loumalouomega/meshioplusplus/blob/main/THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md)). Geogram's constrained tetrahedralisation is in that list too: it creates TetGen's AGPL Delaunay kernel. The separate-process route is `tetrahedralize(backend="tetgen")` and `backend="gmsh"` ([external meshers](./tetrahedralize.md#external-meshers-tetgen-gmsh-and-ftetwild): installed executables behind named tet-mesher providers, never linked), and the rule keeps the gmsh Python API, which runs in-process, out of the interop bridges (4.10).
  - **A native parallel Delaunay kernel** in the style of HXT (Marot, Pellerin and Remacle 2019): it is the fast path of a 3-D mesher the core does not have, and the 2-D triangulation of one face is small enough that faces in parallel already use every thread on a real model.
  - **Advancing-front surface meshing as a second native mesher** (Lo 1985, Peraire et al. 1987, Löhner and Parikh 1988, Schöberl 1997): smoother gradation, but its robustness rests on the front's intersection tests that the Delaunay route avoids, and two native meshers would double what must stay byte-identical. Frontal point placement inside the Delaunay mesher (Rebay 1993, Tristano, Owen and Canann 1998) is the alternative if the varying metric's circumcentres leave triangles poorly aligned on strongly stretched faces.
- **Contact mechanics itself** — penalty or mortar assembly, friction and contact search in time need a live solver. meshio++ detects and exports the interface of one snapshot ([interfaces and contact](./region_adjacency.md)); the contact algorithm belongs in the application that owns the solver.
- **MMG in the base wheel and in the native CLI executables** — it is LGPL-3.0 (GPL-3.0 with the ELAS that `move` needs) and an external install, so the native backend is linked dynamically into builds that opt in, and its operations throw by name where it is absent ([MMG backend](./mmg.md#licence)). v18.0.0 narrowed this for Python: the `meshioplusplus-mmg` add-on wheel ships it, built without ELAS, beside the MIT base wheel ([installation](./installation.md#what-the-pypi-wheels-contain)); the same split for the native CLI, a plugin beside the executable, is queued as 4.8.1. Its WebAssembly half was lifted for the full artifact when roadmap 3.1.8 closed (October 2026): MMG is linked there statically, without ELAS, under the LGPL-3.0 relinking terms the artifact's notices state ([WebAssembly](./wasm.md#mmg-in-the-full-artifact)); the lean artifact keeps none.
- **What the full WebAssembly build leaves, by decision** — recorded when roadmap §5 closed (October 2026); [fit CAD's measured and parked notes](./fit_cad.md#measured-and-parked) hold the numbers and the reopening conditions:
  - **Constraints beyond the coaxial, right-angle and equal-radius ones.** Parallel but offset axes are different surfaces, and a bolt circle or a plane at another angle needs the pattern known to the pass, so `constrain_primitives` stays exact only where it is exact.
  - **G1 at valence 5 and 6 beyond the sweeps' reach.** The refine-before-discard step of the patch network brings the largest G1 angle down on the Catmull-Clark fixtures ([limitations](./fit_cad.md#limitations)); a construction that holds G1 exactly there (Loop and Schaefer's full scheme, or G-splines) is not implemented.
  - **Genus-one-and-more homology cuts.** The layout's pieces are triangle groups, so a cut along both sides of a loop has no representation; that is a change to the layout's partition, not a bounded experiment.
  - **THB stays experimental**, behind its knot-line fallback: a rung whose hierarchical fit misses its target falls back to knot lines, so `"thb"` never leaves as facets a patch `"knots"` fits.
- **KaHIP in the lean WASM build, ADIOS2 and TecIO in either WASM build** — KaHIP ships only in the full artifact (serial kaffpa, built by `build-wasm-deps.sh --full`; a graph partitioner would bloat every consumer's bundle, so the lean pair keeps the SFC method with `"kahip"` throwing by name as the contract); no one has made a wasm build of ADIOS2 (`vtx`); TecIO's redistribution terms keep `szplt` out. Each reports by name in the lean and the full artifact alike ([what stays out](./wasm.md#what-stays-out)).
- **zstd/lz4 codecs in the lean WASM build** — the lean artifact keeps zlib as its only block codec; the full one (v17.2.0) links zstd, lz4 and bzip2. A zstd-compressed VTK XML file is a meshio++ extension VTK itself has no compressor for, so vtk.js does not read it; the second reader of a zstd file is meshio++'s native and Python ones. **Memory mapping and the Kokkos backend under Emscripten** — CMake refuses the Kokkos backend under Emscripten, and there is nothing to map inside MEMFS.
- **Single-file output for DOLFIN, TetGen and EnSight from WASM**, or lifting DOLFIN's simplicial restriction — both are facts of the formats; `writeMesh`'s written-paths return value (v11.2.0) is the fix for the bookkeeping they cause.
- **NURBS patches and trims as a second entity kind inside `Mesh`** — decided with the [isogeometric layer](./iga.md) (v16.43.0, [findings](./spikes.md#the-isogeometric-layer)): a model (`NurbsInfo`, `BrepInfo`) stays a side channel beside the mesh, with its own evaluate, refine and sample operations, because a first-class entity kind needs patches, control nets, weights, knots and trimming curves in `Mesh`, every mesh backend (MESHIO, NATIVE, KRATOS), every binding and the C++ ABI: a major version for a gain the three operations already give. Reopen it when an operation must act on patches and trims as mesh entities (mixed IGA and FEM assembly in one model, for instance, or a patch-aware `partition`), and start from the spike's list of what the side channel cannot do: no `Mesh` operation sees a patch, the model does not travel through the pipeline, and an MFEM model refines every direction alike.
- **Polyhedron blocks as a WASM gap** — `vtu`, `ensight`, `cgns`, `med` and `openfoam` all write them from WASM; a consumer that cannot is constrained by its own data model.
- **Performance work measured or decided against** — recorded when the roadmap's performance section closed (October 2026), and not re-proposed when it reopened as [§5](#performance) in November 2026; a new performance change passes the gates of [landing a performance change](./benchmarks.md#landing-a-performance-change):
  - **The serial phases kept for determinism**: the keep-first weld loop (chains make it order-dependent), the union sweeps of `split` and `compute_normals` (cheap, and their partitions are order-independent anyway), and `remesh`'s energy sweep (every accepted move changes what the next test reads; it has no numpy twin because a near-tie decided differently yields a different clustering).
  - **A facet table cached across pipeline steps** — measured ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#pipeline-row-what-a-shared-facet-table-could-save)): in a `Smooth` then `ExtractSurface` chain a cache saves at most one table build, about 3.6% of the chain on SEQ at tier L with the default ten iterations, at most 10% on four OpenMP threads and about 20% with one iteration. `smooth` and `extract_surface` do not share a record structure, so it would also need a refactor, and a cache on the `Mesh` is a Tier A layout change. Reopen it for a workload that chains several facet readers after cheap topology-preserving steps; the `pipeline_smooth_surface` row measures it.
  - **Link-time optimisation by default** — measured ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#link-time-optimisation)), November 2026: identical output and binaries a third smaller, but the instruction counts move both ways (`read_vtk_ascii` +40%, `gradient` +14%, `decimate` −6%), so it is the opt-in `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_ENABLE_LTO` rather than the default. A profile was tried afterwards (formerly roadmap 5.12) and not adopted either: with a profile from the harness, the median row falls 3.6% in instructions (5.0% with LTO) but 36 rows rise, by up to 49%, and the time of the rows above 30 ms does not move (median ratio 1.008, 1.013 with LTO), identical output throughout ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#profile-guided-optimisation)); `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_PGO` stays as an opt-in two-stage build.
  - **Explicit SIMD intrinsics or `-march` flags** — portability across wheels, WASM and the release binaries is worth more than the scalar kernels cost; revisit only if the harness shows a kernel dominating. Reopened in November 2026 for variants selected at run time inside one portable binary (formerly roadmap 5.15), measured and not taken: base64 decoding is 17% of a zlib-dominated read and encoding is under 1% of a write, the byte swap is 35% of a two-millisecond read, and the number-text and distance kernels are floating-point arithmetic, not bit-identical comparisons ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#which-byte-level-kernels-dominate-a-read-vector-kernels-measured)). Reopen it for a workload where an uncompressed base64 or big-endian payload is the bulk of the time.
  - **Kokkos device execution** — every `parallel_for` body captures host pointers (`parallel.hpp`); the GPU route is the DLPack/CuPy handoff ([GPU handoff](./gpu.md)).
  - **An occupancy pyramid over the distance query's bucket grid** (formerly roadmap 5.3.1) — measured in November 2026 and not taken: descending the grid's occupancy at coarser block sizes, best first with Euclidean bounds, was 2 to 3 times faster than the Chebyshev-shell search at tier L, but a bounding-volume hierarchy of the triangles (formerly 5.3.2, which had been decided against because its visit order was thought to make the equidistant-triangle tiebreak observable, a reason the total order on (distance², triangle id) answers) was a further 2 to 4 times faster with its build included, on uniform and graded targets alike, and replaced the grid in ABI 40 ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#a-bounding-volume-hierarchy-for-the-distance-query-and-a-grid-for-find_interface)). A cell's bound is the cell's, so a point far from the surface still opens every bucket of a patch around its nearest one; a box is as tight as its triangles.
  - **Tuning the pure-Python fallback readers** — the fix is making the C++ path accept the file, not a faster fallback; `_decimate.py`'s heap-based twin is deleted once `decimate` accepts its inputs, not optimised.
  - **Dropping the copy in `transform`** — `transform` copies the connectivity and data it does not change (through `transform_owned_copy`, as 17 operation files copy through `clone_mesh`, `clone_geometry` or `quality_clone_mesh`), and the Python twin copies too; that copy is what makes the result a new mesh that aliases nothing, so removing it is an API change, not an optimisation. Narrowed in November 2026 to a second, explicitly in-place entry point (formerly roadmap 5.14), then measured and not taken: at tier L the three `transform` calls of the `pipeline_transform_chain` row are at most 2.5% of it even counted as copies whole, the `vtu` write 88%, a whole clone 0.3%, and the data-only operations (`data_rename`, `data_drop`, `data_calc`) under 1% of the write each ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#what-an-in-place-transform-could-save)). Reopen it for a workload that chains transforms without a write, on a mesh where that share is shown to matter.
  - **Fields-only reads in sequences** (formerly roadmap 5.11.2: a `ReadOptions` flag so the in-file-timeline readers skip a static geometry after the first step) — measured in November 2026 and not taken: on a 12-step series of 162,000 tetrahedra the geometry is 66% of an XDMF step's read (0.9 of 1.4 ms) and 88% of a VTKHDF step's (4.1 of 4.6 ms), and a whole step of an XDMF-to-VTU conversion takes 39.8 ms, so skipping it saves about 2% (XDMF) to 9% (VTKHDF) of the cheapest pipeline there is and nothing measurable once an operation or a heavier writer runs ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#read-ahead-in-sequences)). It would add a flag to `ReadOptions` (the padding has room for one byte, not for a mesh pointer) and a data-onto-geometry merge in both drivers, for four readers. Exodus has no series writer to build the series with, so it was not measured. Reopen it for a workload where reading is the bulk of a step, such as a very large mesh read many times with no writer.
  - **Caching between the browser viewer's applies** — every apply re-reads the staged file and replays the whole pipeline, which is what makes undo exact. Measured with the typed-array channel ([benchmarks](./benchmarks.md#a-typed-array-channel-for-the-browser-viewer)), the read is at most 2 to 25% of an apply that smooths and up to 69% of a quality-only apply on a large surface; a cached mesh or pipeline prefix would hold a second copy of the mesh in the 32-bit heap MEMFS already shares, and make undo depend on invalidation. The heap's size is the half of this a higher ceiling or a 64-bit build would change (5.13); measure again there before reopening.

- **Code from AGPL-3.0 scan tools** — [ScanRuler](https://github.com/CNCKitchen/ScanRuler) and its STEP importer meshStep are AGPL-3.0, which this MIT library cannot take in any form: no copying, porting, translating or paraphrasing of their code, tests, shaders, guides or data. The scan metrology section (then §7, closed November 2026) was listed from their published feature descriptions only; an agent implementing an item of that kind (9.9 is one) works from the literature it cites and from meshio++'s own kernels, does not open their source, and does not use their output files as fixtures. The same rule as for Triangle, TetGen and Gmsh above, without the separate-process exception, since nothing there needs to run them.
- **Booleans of a scan with fitted solids** — subtracting a measured cylinder from a scan, or adding a modelled body to it (roadmap 7.8.7, closed November 2026), waits on surface booleans, which a spike measured and kept out: exact in topology, but rounding its output to doubles left most curved results self-intersecting and coplanar contact unhandled ([findings](./spikes.md#surface-booleans-formerly-roadmap-7-8)). A fitted solid reaches a CAD kernel as STEP ([`write_step_elements`](./formats/step.md#elements)), where the boolean is the kernel's. If a boolean backend passes the spike's criteria (9.1), this is its first use and the entry is removed.
- **An interactive parametric CAD modeller** — a sketch constraint solver, a feature history with rebuilds and rollback, face fingerprints that survive upstream edits, thread and insert catalogues: a CAD application, not a mesh library. meshio++ fits and writes geometry ([`fit_cad`](./fit_cad.md), 7.6.5) and may build a solid from a scripted list of profile features through the CAD provider (7.7.4); editing that solid belongs to the CAD tool that opens the STEP file.
- **Terminal rendering stays software, dependency-free and core-only** — no GPU, no windowing stack, no new dependency and no audio library; it is a *preview*, not a replacement for Polyscope or vtk.js, and does not try to match their fidelity, lighting or picking. Python calls the core or raises by name (the [`remesh_volume`](./remesh_volume.md) precedent: a NumPy edge-function rasterizer would be slow and double the surface that has to stay byte-identical). Vector output is unchanged: SVG, TikZ and glTF keep the painter's algorithm and their byte-pinned twins, and their colormap tables are only ever extended. The interactive loop is not a library feature (raw mode, signals and the event loop live in the CLI layer; the library exposes frames and encoders, never a tty, and WASM and the C API carry frames and text only). `--follow` polls modification times and does not use inotify or kqueue. The soundtrack is generated, played by an external player and silent unless asked for, in CI and over SSH. See [terminal rendering](./tui.md#design-decisions).

---

## Suggested sequencing

Open work only; what shipped is in `CHANGELOG.md`.

1. **Correctness debts ([§1](#correctness-debts))** — decoding the both-flags `.rst` records (1.1.2) needs a DPF Server and its example files, so it waits on 2.2.3's installation.
2. **Format reach ([§2](#format-reach))** — the checks against the vendor tools, as licences or files the tools wrote appear ([recording a run](#awaiting-a-licensed-run)). Depth before breadth in what is new, smallest first: writing the EnSight parts that were read (2.3.3.1), then CGNS's boundary conditions and structured zones (2.3.1.1, 2.3.1.3), Exodus's globals (2.3.2.1) and MOAB H5M element tags and DAGMC sets (2.3.10). Of 2.4, the shared netCDF helper (2.4.3.1) and the ZIP container (2.4.5.1) are prerequisites worth having on their own; Fluent's case files (2.4.1), the coastal trio (2.4.3) and the high-order CFD solvers PyFR and Nek5000/NekRS (2.4.9) follow.
3. **The OSS-Fuzz submission (3.1) and registration ([4.1](#ecosystem-reach))** — calendar-bound, so start them early. Among the registries, conda-forge (4.1.1), Blender (4.1.4) and the container image (4.1.8) need no decision; CRAN (4.1.2) and Julia (4.1.3) need the build question settled first. The dataset manager's thumbnails (4.6) can run beside them. The hosted MCP server's first steps (4.18.1 the image, 4.18.2 Glama, 4.18.3 the official registry) need no decision and go with them; the public endpoint (4.18.7) waits on its design pass.
4. **Tooling that guards everything after it ([§3](#quality-of-implementation))** — in this order, each days of work: `clang-format` and the documentation checks (3.8, 3.16), coverage on pull requests and the SonarCloud low-coverage hotspots (3.11.1, 3.11.5), the compiler cache (3.17.2), the warning option and its first count (3.6.1, 3.6.2), ThreadSanitizer (3.9.1), the C API `abidiff` gate (3.13.1). Then the longer ones: warnings to zero (3.6.3), CodeQL (3.10.1), typed Python (3.7), the round-trip fuzz target (3.12.1, after 3.1), the Clang-Tidy stages (3.5), structural decomposition of the complexity monoliths (3.24, paired with 3.17.3) and the free-threaded wheel (3.15.1, after 3.9.1). Code cleaning (3.4) runs alongside everything, in the order SonarCloud's duplication report gives.
5. **Performance ([§5](#performance))** — the wheels' two checks (5.2), the 64-bit WebAssembly build with the caching re-measurement (5.13), and (on profile) the HDF5 lock split (5.8.2); streaming writers (5.16) sit with large-data work.
6. **Split Python packages on Windows ([4.7](#ecosystem-reach)) and the CLI plugins (4.8)** — weigh the C-ABI plugin contract (4.19) alongside the static-runtime measurement (4.8.1.2), since a C99 plugin boundary resolves both the static Linux CLI and the Windows `/MT` CLI (4.7.2) in one design; the MSVC library recipe (4.7.1) is independent.
7. **Bindings and front ends** — Rust (4.2) with the C-header parity guard, which Julia and R gain from too; then .NET (4.11). Zero-copy Arrow C Data Interface (4.20) serves Python, C, C++, R, Julia and Rust at once. The Jupyter widget (4.12) and the VS Code preview (4.13) share the viewer's build and the rebuild of its embedded page (7.3). Interop phases 2 and 3 (4.3, 4.10) are days each.
8. **The chatbot (4.4) and the embeddable MCP server (4.9)** — the schema generator (4.4.2) first, since both need it; then 4.9's design pass before any native code, since it decides whether the Python server's sandbox and sanitizer move into the core.
9. **Code duplication ([§6](#code-duplication))** — finish the remaining `svg`/`tikz` drawing writer internals (6.2) and rerun `tools/dup_report.py` alongside everything, like 3.4.
10. **SonarCloud findings ([§7](#sonarcloud-findings))** — confirm the 45 fixes and the coverage path on the next `master` scan (7.1, 7.2), then make the gates required (7.4) and add the viewer's rebuild check (7.3) with the next WebAssembly build.
11. **Operations ([§9](#operations))** — the boolean backend's measurement first (9.1.1, 9.1.2), since it decides a non-goal and two parked features; then the small, high-leverage ones (all-hits ray queries 9.3.1, averaged voxel point and `subsample_points` 9.9.1/9.9.2, the IGA list 9.8); then cancellation/progress (9.16), subdivision and elevation (9.2, 9.5), partitioner providers (9.7) and repeated green refinement (9.6.1). Only 9.10.2 and 9.6.2 wait on a decision.
12. **Data model ([§8](#data-model))** — design passes only until one says otherwise. The two whose recommended form is a convention and days of work, and which close losses already listed on format pages: complex pairs (8.1) and the structured annotation (8.6). Strings (8.2) before hierarchy (8.5), which uses them. Integration-point data (8.3) is the largest and the one most formats wait on (2.3.6.1, MED ELGA, the result readers); side data (8.4) unblocks 2.3.1.4 and 2.3.2.3. The format items that wait on each say so.
13. **In-situ interchange, the modern C++ surface and provenance ([§10](#in-situ-interchange) to [§12](#provenance-remote))** — after everything above, in this order by dependency: the Conduit bridge (10.1) in Python first, since it needs no core change, and the ghost conventions (10.4) with 2.3.8.1, which wants the same names; errors as values and views (11.1, 11.2) together in one ABI change, then the concepts on top (11.3); PROV and RO-Crate (12.1), the `dataset add` statistics (12.3) and the remote-read decision (12.2) are small and independent. The upstream-meshio run (3.18) and the identifier-width audit (3.19) belong with the tooling of item 4. §10 to §12 came from a proposed revision of this file in November 2026: what the revision listed that the tree or this file already had (supply chain, hardening, the ABI diff, the C++ module, Zarr and pmsh in the core, the dataset manifests, the PyG dataset, partition ghosts and the `pvtu` writer, node-order tables, fsspec URLs, the mutation-testing and engine-differential spikes) was not repeated.
14. **The remaining proposal-derived items** — the MSH 4.1 sections (2.3.6.3), QA records (2.3.2.5), collated OpenFOAM (2.3.5.4), VTKHDF composites (2.3.7.5) and glTF and OpenUSD completeness (2.3.9.8, 2.3.9.9) with format reach; the nanobind spike (4.17) beside 3.15.1; solver adapters (4.16) after 4.3 and 4.10; streaming writers (5.16); the resource-limit audit (12.4) beside 3.12; required gates (7.4) after 7.1 and 7.2.
15. **Educational animation ([§13](#manim))** — the frame-stability and version check of 13.6.2 first, since every later step is rendered in CI or not at all, and the decisions of 13.1 with it; then the payload (13.2), which needs no manim, and `to_manim` with the helpers (13.3.1, 13.3.2) on top. The animations (13.4) are written as the pilot episode (13.5.1) asks for them, not ahead of it; the remaining episodes follow in the table's order, each alone. The raster path (13.3.5) is written when the first episode that needs a mesh above `max_faces` does; smooth shading and the OpenGL or ManimGL route (13.3.4, 13.3.6) and the ManimGL leg (13.6.3) wait on 13.6.4's measurements. Independent of every other section, so it runs beside any of the above.
16. **The paper ([§14](#paper))** — the decisions (14.1) at once, because consent, endorsement and a venue's policy on AI assistance are calendar-bound and the last can change what kind of paper is possible; the claims table and the attribution (14.2.1, 14.2.2) next, since they say what the experiments must show; the bibliography (14.3) and the build (14.6) can start the same day. The experiments (14.4) are run once, at the release 14.1.7 fixes, after which the generated numbers (14.5.3) are frozen at that tag. A paper is better for the registrations of 4.1 being done (its availability section) and for stills from §13 (14.5.4), but waits on neither. Posting (14.7.2) is an author's act.

### Milestones

By theme, not by version number: the project bumps its major version on an ABI change, so a version here would be a guess.

| Milestone | Content | Exit criterion |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Format leftovers | 1.1.2, 2.3.1 to 2.3.6, 2.3.9, 2.3.10, 2.4.9 | `cgnscheck`, `exodiff`, `mbsize` and `checkMesh` clean on written files; no fixture fails as a pointer |
| Quality gate | 6.2, 7.1, 7.2, 7.4, 3.11.5 | SonarCloud quality gate green (A/A) |
| A stable C++ surface | 11.1, 11.2 in one ABI change, then 11.3, 4.19, 9.16 | `try_read` on junk bytes returns an `Error` for every registered format under ASan; pure-C99 plugin loads into static CLIs; Ctrl-C interrupts long ops |
| In-situ and large data | 10.1, 10.2, 10.4, 2.3.7.5, 4.20, 5.16, 12.4 | Blueprint `verify` passes for every cell type; Arrow C Data zero-copy verified; a 10⁸-cell streamed write stays under its memory bound |
| Reach | 4.1, 4.8, 4.16, 3.18, 12.1 to 12.3 | upstream meshio's suite runs with a known-failures file; a Poisson solve per landed adapter |
| Research | 4.17, 3.11.3, the long spikes of [spikes](./spikes.md) | a written decision per spike |
| Outreach | 13.1 to 13.6, 14.1 to 14.7 | the pilot episode embedded on the docs site and rendered in CI; the arXiv identifier in `CITATION.cff`, its tier S numbers reproduced from the tag |

| VTK mesh-aware math | Kitware's *Math on Meshes* (kitware.com/math-on-meshes); docs.vtk.org, `vtkmodules.numpy_interface.algorithms`, `vtkCellDerivatives`, `vtkMatrixMathFilter`, `vtkGradientFilter`, the CellGrid filters |
| Verdict quality metrics | Stimpson et al., *The Verdict Geometric Quality Library*, Sandia report SAND2007-1751 |
| Vortex criteria | Hunt, Wray and Moin 1988 (Q); Jeong and Hussain 1995 (λ2), *J. Fluid Mech.* 285 |
| Discrete exterior calculus, heat method | Desbrun, Hirani, Leok and Marsden, *Discrete Exterior Calculus* (arXiv math/0508341); Crane, *Discrete Differential Geometry: An Applied Introduction*; Crane, Weischedel and Wardetzky 2013 (heat method) |
| Bilateral / guided mesh denoising | Fleishman, Drori and Cohen-Or 2003 (*SIGGRAPH*); Sun et al. 2007 (*IEEE TVCG*); Zheng et al. 2011 (*IEEE TVCG*); Zhang et al. 2015 (*IEEE TVCG*) |
| Mean-curvature flow skeletonization | Au et al. 2008 (*SIGGRAPH*); Tagliasacchi et al. 2012 (*SGP*) |
| Exact geodesic paths and flip-based geodesics | Sharp and Crane 2020 (*SIGGRAPH Asia*, *You Can Find Geodesic Paths in Triangle Meshes by Just Flipping Edges*) |
| FFD and RBF mesh morphing | Sederberg and Parry 1986 (*SIGGRAPH*); de Boer, van der Schoot and Bijl 2007 (*Computers & Structures* 85) |
| Preconditioned LOBPCG | Knyazev 2001 (*SIAM J. Sci. Comput.* 23) |
| Spectral shape descriptors (HKS, WKS) | Sun, Ovsjanikov and Guibas 2009 (*SGP*); Aubry, Schlickewei and Cremers 2011 (*ICCV Workshops*) |
| Runge–Kutta step control | Dormand and Prince 1980, *J. Comput. Appl. Math.* 6 |
